# CLAUDE.md — ibms-app

This is the first IBMS engineering repo. **Standards, mandatory rules, domain
knowledge, and architecture decisions live in `ibms-brain` (imported above via git
submodule at `ibms-brain/`), not here** — `ibms-brain/meta/lex/` (mandatory),
`ibms-brain/meta/context/` (domain knowledge), `ibms-brain/meta/designs/` (why decisions
were made). Read the relevant `meta/context/` file before touching an area, and the
`meta/lex/` rules before any non-trivial change — most of them (money-decimal-jod,
workflow-state-transitions, maker-checker-segregation, sensitive-data-handling,
pdpl-sla-timers) apply the moment real domain code lands here, which it has not yet.

## What's New

| 2026-10-20 | **CI WENT RED AND THE CAUSE WAS MINE; THEN THE FIX'S OWN RULE WAS APPLIED ONE SWEEP SHORT.** **(1) THE RED.** `backend` failed on `9da34f1` with **eleven leaked organizations** and the harness's own message naming the diagnosis. The chain: decision 25's invitation issues a `PasswordResetToken`, `PasswordResetToken_userId_fkey` is not a cascade, two fixture teardowns spelled `user.deleteMany({ where: { organizationId } })` inline, so the delete threw inside `afterAll`, the organization survived, and every later spec failed at `createTestApp` with a fault that looks like its own. **Exactly two specs invite** and so carry a token in a disposable organization — measured, not assumed. **WHY IT WAS INVISIBLE LOCALLY, which is the part to keep**: I read the run output through `grep -E "Tests |Test Files"` and an `afterAll` error is neither of those lines; and the local leak was HARMLESS to the harness while CI's was fatal, because `assertOneOrganization` counts BROKERAGE OFFICES and the company spec leaks `INSURANCE_COMPANY` rows. Same root cause, opposite visibility. Fixed through ONE shared `deleteFixtureUsersInOrganization`, clearing the five dependents in order. **And the leaked rows had become UNDELETABLE**, blocked by `SlaHoliday_organizationId_fkey`: re-running `db:test:seed` for an unrelated change made `ensureJordanFixedHolidays` write sixteen holiday rows into each leaked fixture. db-test back to 2 organizations, 21 removed, **none refused for audit rows** — the cleanup refused on that condition rather than deleting around it. **(2) AND THE RULE I WROTE FOR IT WAS WRONG BY ONE SWEEP.** The comment said "branches, departments and holidays", which is **two sweeps counted as three models**: `organization.findMany` appears THREE times in `seed.ts` and the third, `ensureBrokerLicenseTerms`, writes a licence plus its certificate `Document` — and the cascade cleared neither. It had not fired only because eleven of the twelve leaks were `INSURANCE_COMPANY` and that sweep filters on `BROKERAGE_OFFICE`. **The TYPE spared us, not the code.** Measured OFF THE DATABASE: the chain is deeper than reported — `BrokerLicenseScopeLine_brokerLicenseId_fkey` is a third RESTRICT level under the licence. **THE CERTIFICATE IS NOT DELETED JUST BECAUSE A LICENCE POINTED AT IT**: a `Document` has six inbound FKs, so removal requires BOTH that its own `organizationId` is this organization AND that nothing outside the removed licences references it; either failing THROWS with the document named, because a teardown that deleted somebody else's evidence to tidy up would be worse than the leak. **(3) THE RULE IS A DERIVATION NOW, NOT A COMMENT** — `npm run check:seed-sweeps`, a `backend` CI step before the migrations. It reads the sweeps out of `seed.ts` and the cleared models out of `tenant-prisma.ts`, and **DERIVES THE DENOMINATOR**: every occurrence of the sweep shape is ATTRIBUTED to the top-level `async function` containing it, and an unattributable occurrence **FAILS BY LINE**. The floors were minimums — they catch a parser that stopped working, not a fourth sweep in a shape the parser cannot read. The attribution is total rather than partial on a measurement: `prisma/seed.ts` is the only seed entry point, `db:seed` and `db:test:seed` both resolving to it. **FOUR DEFECTS IN THE GATE, EVERY ONE FOUND BY A PLANT OR BY ITS OWN FLOORS:** a model named only in a COMMENT read as "cleared" (this repository's own rule biting inside the gate built to enforce it); the attribution was POSITIONAL, so a planted arrow-function sweep was absorbed into the preceding function and reported as attributed — it failed, but for the wrong reason, and would have passed had the planted sweep written an already-cleared model; the body brace was taken as the first `{` after the name, which lands inside `): Promise<{ … }> {`; and the `;`-means-no-body guard fired on the semicolon INSIDE that same return type, so the helper file parsed to **zero** functions. The non-vacuity floor refused on the last two rather than reporting a sweep with no writes — **its third catch of a parser defect**. And the first line number it printed was **944 for a sweep at 1326**, because comment stripping deleted lines: a number nobody can re-derive cannot carry a rule, and the gate is not exempt, so stripping now preserves every position. **THE GATE'S LIMIT IS STATED RATHER THAN DISCOVERED**: it proves a model is cleared SOMEWHERE, not that the rows go. A plant emptying the licence array while leaving `brokerLicense.deleteMany` in place **passes the gate and dies in the e2e** — both plants committed, and the division of labour is the point. **(4) PROVEN TWICE, INCLUDING LIVE WITH THE REAL SEED.** `fixture-cascade.e2e-spec.ts` attaches exactly what the three sweeps attach, asserts a bare organization delete is **REFUSED first** so the cascade test is not vacuous, then proves the cascade removes everything. And live: a brokerage-office fixture leaked deliberately, `db:test:seed` re-run, 1 branch + 1 department + 8 holidays attaching, a bare delete refused, then the cascade leaving zero rows. **That run NARROWED the finding rather than confirming it whole** — `ensureBrokerLicenseTerms` skips an office with NO USER, because `Document.uploadedByUserId` is NOT NULL and it needs an uploader, so the licence pair attaches only to a leaked office that HAS one, which is exactly what the registration spec's invitation creates. **AND THE NEW SPEC WAS ITSELF A LEAK**, caught by the harness at once: it created a second BROKERAGE_OFFICE and both sibling specs refused at `createTestApp` naming it while the file itself passed. Its fixture is an `INSURANCE_COMPANY` now — the licence chain is attached by hand and that FK carries no type condition, so the rows and the RESTRICTs behave identically — and it sweeps its own leftovers on ENTRY, **by hand rather than through the helper under test**, because a spec that heals itself through the thing it is testing cannot heal the one run that needs it. **Verification**: api typecheck 0 errors; api lint 0 errors; the gate green at 3 occurrences all attributed and 7 models cleared; three plants each behaving as declared; the three affected specs **36/36 run TWICE CONSECUTIVELY** with the organization count measured at 2 after each; **287 committed plants across 69 files** apply. | **`npm run check:seed-sweeps` IS A CI GATE: EVERY PER-ORGANIZATION SEED SWEEP MUST BE CLEARED BY `deleteOrganizationCascade`.** A disposable fixture organization is one of the rows those sweeps walk, every FK involved is RESTRICT, and one seed run after a leak makes that fixture PERMANENTLY undeletable — which takes down every later spec at `createTestApp` with a fault that looks like its own. This happened twice. **A FIXTURE TEARDOWN MUST USE `deleteFixtureUsersInOrganization`, NEVER `user.deleteMany` SPELLED OUT** — the order has one home, and decision 25's `PasswordResetToken` is why. **THE GATE PROVES A MODEL IS CLEARED SOMEWHERE, NOT THAT THE ROWS GO** — `fixture-cascade.e2e-spec.ts` is the other half, and a cascade that mentions a model while clearing nothing passes the gate by design. **READ A RUN'S WHOLE OUTPUT, NOT A GREP OF IT**: an `afterAll` error matches neither `Tests ` nor `Test Files`, and that is how eleven leaked organizations reached CI. **A NEW SPEC MUST NOT CREATE A SECOND `BROKERAGE_OFFICE`** — `createTestApp` asserts exactly one, so it fails every concurrently-running spec for as long as the fixture exists; use `INSURANCE_COMPANY` unless the office type is what is under test. **AND SWEEP ON ENTRY BY HAND, NOT THROUGH THE HELPER UNDER TEST.** |

After item (f) I found a single pre-existing TS2719 in `employee.config.spec.ts`, established it reproduces on an unmodified HEAD, and then used `build` as my compile check for the rest of the session — so CI found **six errors across five files** that my local gates could not see. `commission-agreement.service.spec.ts` passed a string at 16 call sites after item (h) widened `create` to take the full caller, in **TWO SPELLINGS of the same fixture** (`'mgr-1'` and `'actor-1'`), which is why my first pass fixed 11 and missed 5; `policy.service.spec.ts`, `rfq.service.spec.ts` and `duty-segregation-readiness.spec.ts` (twice) were short the `InsuranceLineRepository` argument; and `employee-cbj-approval.e2e-spec.ts` typed a role as `RoleName`, **the legacy enum from before office-scoped roles**, which cannot name `OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR` — that spec PASSED at runtime because vitest compiles through swc, which strips types instead of checking them, the same trap `sample:release` hit earlier today. **The lesson is not "run typecheck" — it is that WORKING AROUND A BROKEN GATE IS HOW A GATE STOPS CATCHING THINGS.** **CORRECTION, and it is the sharpest lesson of the three: the TS2719 WAS MINE AND CI DOES REPORT IT.** I wrote here that "CI's own typecheck reports ZERO TS2719" — FALSE. I had read CI's error list through `head -14` and the TS2719 sat below the cut: the head-truncated-grep failure this repo has already recorded three times, this time used to justify a claim in writing. And my "it reproduces on unmodified HEAD" test was CONTAMINATED — I stashed the source but `node_modules/.prisma/client` still held item (g)'s regenerated columns, so the stashed spec compiled against a client from the future. **The actual cause was item (g):** `employee.config.spec.ts`'s fixture uses the FULL `Employee` model type precisely so a new column cannot be added without a decision being made there — its own header says so — and it fired exactly as designed while I called it an artefact. Adding `cbjApprovalStatus` and `cbjApprovalDate` takes `npm run typecheck` to **6/6, zero errors**. Two wrong diagnoses, both from an experiment I did not control. **AND I SHIPPED THE DRIFT CLASS ITEM (g) IS ABOUT, IN ITEM (g).** `person-and-login.spec.ts` mocks the employee detail read and did not carry `cbjApproval`, so the panel's `approval.status` threw during render — taking down the reveal, the training list, the correction form and the de-provisioning checklist with it. I updated `employees.spec.ts`'s fixture because I was editing that file, and never asked which OTHER specs mock the same endpoint. Measured properly: **four specs mock an employee detail read**; `employee-reveal.spec.ts` goes to `/employees/reveal` (a different page, which is why it passed) and `users.spec.ts` only asserts a link, so one fixture needed the field. **The panel now DEGRADES rather than taking the page down**, as a decision rather than a patch: it renders "the server did not send this" and NOT "no approval on record", because those are different facts and the second would be a wrong claim about a person's regulatory standing — the api e2e still guarantees the field is sent, this only bounds the damage, and a Playwright test asserts BOTH halves (the notice appears AND the rest of the page survives). **Verification**: api typecheck down to the one known error from five files of them; api lint and build exit 0; api unit **255 files / 3263 tests** exit 0; api e2e 2 files / 5 tests; web typecheck and lint exit 0; web unit **136 tests**; Playwright employees + person-and-login + employee-reveal + users **40/40**, and the FULL web suite **759/760** — the one failure is `permission-refusal.spec.ts:157`, which passes in isolation and is contention in a 760-test run, not a finding. | **`npm run build -w api` IS NOT A TYPECHECK** — `tsconfig.build.json` excludes specs, so a widened service signature or a changed constructor is invisible to it. There is no tolerated baseline: the one I thought was tolerated was my own defect (see the CORRECTION above). **Never conclude "CI does not report X" from a `head`-truncated error list**, and never test "does this pre-exist" by stashing source while leaving a regenerated Prisma client in place — the stashed code then compiles against a client from the future. **Never work around a gate that has a standing error — establish what the baseline is and read past it**, because substituting a weaker gate is how six errors reached CI. **A vitest-passing spec is not a type-checked spec**: swc strips types. **`ensureRole` takes a plain `string`, not `RoleName`** — that enum predates office-scoped roles and cannot name `OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR`. **When an api view gains a field a screen reads, grep for EVERY spec that mocks that endpoint** — four do for the employee detail — and widen them all in the same change. **A component reading a hand-copied HTTP field should not take its whole page down**: render a distinct "the server did not send this" state, never a default that makes a false claim. |

do not paraphrase it** — `/auth/mfa/totp/enroll` and `/auth/mfa/totp/enroll/verify` were invented as `/auth/mfa/enroll` and `/auth/mfa/verify`, and both tests failed with a bare "expected 201, got 404" that named no request. **Assert money with `.toFixed(3)`**, the repo's convention: `Prisma.Decimal.toString()` trims trailing zeros, so 13 assertions compared `'0'` to `'0.000'`. **A new org-carrying table gets `ENABLE` AND `FORCE` RLS** — measured, `FORCE` is the convention for every table added since 2026-11-02 (four of them), and the 126 older ones predate it. |
| 2026-10-11 | **MODULE 0 ITEM (d) — THE EXPIRY RULE, AND A LICENCE THAT SURVIVES ITS OWN PRINTED EXPIRY BY OPERATION OF LAW.** No migration: item (d) is pure rules, five derived view fields and three screen notices. **ART. 10/b — A TERM ENDS 31 DECEMBER, AND A DATE THAT IS NOT ONE WARNS RATHER THAN REFUSING.** The date is entered as printed on the certificate and the certificate is the authority, so `expiryIsEndOfCalendarYear` drives a `role="status"` notice telling the admin to verify, and the entry stands — the Playwright test asserts the PAIR (the warning is present AND the term is still usable), because a test that only checked the warning would pass against an implementation that also blocked. **The three-calendar-years half is surfaced and deliberately NOT a second warning condition**: `expectedExpiryForThreeYearTerm` is informational, because whether a term issued 2026-06-15 should end 2028-12-31 or 2029-12-31 is genuinely ambiguous and a question for the regulator, not something to infer and then warn on. Inventing a warning the brief did not ask for is how a screen starts crying wolf. **ART. 11 — 45 CALENDAR DAYS, AND THE READING IS A DECISION.** Not business days: the deadline is a span before a fixed calendar date, and counting it in business days would move it EARLIER — making an office late for a filing the regulation calls timely. That is the same failure direction as the empty holiday calendar, where the error ran against the brokerage, so where a reading is ambiguous the literal one wins. `renewalApplicationDueBy` is plain millisecond arithmetic and a test crosses a LEAP-YEAR February on purpose (45 days before 2028-03-15 is 2028-01-30, where month arithmetic would give 01-31 and be wrong by a day in exactly the direction that reports an on-time filing as late). `renewalFiledOnTime` is true ON the deadline, not merely before it — asserted, because `<` instead of `<=` would make an office filing exactly on time late. **THE BY-OPERATION-OF-LAW EXTENSION.** After expiry an office that applied on time stays valid until the CBJ's decision issues; one that filed nothing, or filed late, expires. **"Until the decision issues" needs NO extra column**, and that is the design rather than a shortcut: the decision IS the next term being recorded, and a later term wins `pickCurrentTerm` the moment it exists — so the branch is only ever reached for the office's LATEST term, which by definition has no decision after it. A `renewalDecidedAt` column would be a second home for a fact already derivable from the series, free to drift from it. **A MANUAL LAPSE OUTRANKS THE EXTENSION, and the ORDERING IS ASSERTED**: a CBJ suspension is not cured by having applied for renewal, so the `status === 'lapsed'` check comes first; planting the inversion kills two named tests. **`validByOperationOfLaw` IS ITS OWN FLAG rather than folded into `isCurrentlyLapsed`**, because the two answer different questions and a screen needs both — "may this office place business" (it may) and "is this term past its printed expiry" (it is). Collapsed, an officer would see an expiry in the past beside a system that is content and nothing explaining why. **PLANTS, AND ONE OF THEM TAUGHT ME SOMETHING ABOUT PLANTING.** Dropping the renewal-pending rule kills exactly three tests — the pure rule, the view, and the actual placement gate — so the rule is observed at all three layers rather than only where it is written. Inverting the manual-lapse ordering kills two. **A dropped wire field reproduces this project's documented signature exactly**: making the view stop sending `renewalDueBy` while keeping its TYPE intact (so TypeScript cannot see it) fails the api e2e with `expected undefined to be '2124-11-16T00:00:00.000Z'` — the §1.80 shape, and the only thing that catches it is the HTTP assertion, because the screen reads these five fields through a hand-copied interface. **AND MY FIRST SCREEN PLANT PROVED NOTHING**: deleting the warning's whole JSX block killed ALL EIGHT Playwright tests, because it broke the page rather than disabling the notice. Changing the CONDITION to `false` kills exactly one, 7 pass. **A plant must change the MECHANISM, not the surrounding structure — a plant that breaks the page cannot tell you which test observes what.** **AND A TEST OF MINE WAS INSENSITIVE, found by the plant rather than by reading it.** The policy-level "a MANUAL lapse outranks a timely filing" test used a FUTURE expiry, so the ordering it claims to be about never mattered — the inversion plant left it green while its sibling in the config spec died. Expired + manually lapsed + filed on time is the only combination where the ordering decides the answer; re-pointed there, the plant kills both. | **Art. 11 is 45 CALENDAR days** (`RENEWAL_APPLICATION_DUE_DAYS_BEFORE_EXPIRY`), and `renewalFiledOnTime` is inclusive of the deadline. Do not convert it to business days without a regulator's reading: it would move the deadline earlier and report timely filings as late. **A non-31-December expiry WARNS, never blocks** — the certificate is the authority. Do not promote the three-calendar-years expectation into a second warning; it is ambiguous mid-year and is surfaced for a human to read. **`isBrokerLicenseCurrentlyLapsed` now takes `renewalRequestedAt` as well**, and its check ORDER is load-bearing: manual lapse, then inside-the-term, then the renewal-pending rule — which is the OWNER'S DECISION and NOT art. 11, corrected 2026-10-12: that article is one sentence about a 45-day filing deadline and contains no extension. **Do not add a `renewalDecidedAt` column** — the next term IS the decision, and `pickCurrentTerm` already prefers it. **`validByOperationOfLaw` and `isCurrentlyLapsed` are not the same question**; a screen needs both. **When a view gains a field the screen reads, assert it ON THE RESPONSE BODY in an api e2e** — the web's interfaces are hand-copied over HTTP and the compiler cannot check them; planting the field away is invisible to `tsc` and caught only there. **A PLANT MUST DISABLE A MECHANISM, NOT DELETE A BLOCK**: deleting JSX broke the page and killed all 8 tests, which proves nothing about isolation. **And revert a plant with an ABSOLUTE path** — a relative `cp` after a `cd` failed silently twice today and left the plant live in the tree both times. |

Seeding only the default office would have reproduced the exact defect being removed and hidden it from every walkthrough. **GATED ON `SEED_SAMPLE_DATA` (`NODE_ENV !== "production"`), and that gate is load-bearing rather than tidy**: a placeholder licence in production would hand an office the one thing the new gate exists to withhold, and would then report every unlicensed office as licensed — a worse failure than the one this item fixed, because it would look like compliance. The number says `CBJ-SEED-…` and the certificate says `not-a-real-cbj-document` so neither can be mistaken for real. **THE STALE TEST WAS REPOINTED AND A SECOND ONE ADDED, because one test changing meaning is not one test per behaviour.** `policy.service.spec.ts`'s *"proceeds when no BrokerLicense record is configured (not an oversight — see assertLicenseNotLapsed)"* encoded the old behaviour as intended; it is renamed *"REFUSES placement when the office holds no licence term — this used to proceed"* with the reversal stated in a comment, and a separate test asserts the OTHER behaviour — that no Policy is created and the refusal **cites art. 21/a/9**, because a 422 that does not say why sends Compliance hunting a system fault. Planting the old `if (!license) return;` back kills **exactly those two and no others** (59 pass), so each is independently observing its own rule. **THE SPEC'S DEFAULT HAD TO INVERT TOO**: `makeDeps()` used to mean "no licence, not blocked", which is what let every other `place` test ignore the licence; it now means a VALID term, with `brokerLicense: null` the explicit opt-in to absence. **THE SCREEN WAS THE REAL DELIVERABLE AND IT CARRIED THE DRIFT CLASS.** `lib/compliance-risk/broker-license-api.ts` hand-copies its interfaces over an HTTP response, which is precisely how the customer-import result panel broke (two fields replaced by one, both read as `undefined`, a throw during render, nothing failing at compile time) — so the client, the screen, the i18n keys in BOTH languages and the Playwright mock all moved in this commit. `GET /broker-license` now answers `{ current, terms }` and **no longer 404s** for an office with no licence: that is a real state the screen must render AND the state that blocks new business, and a 404 is indistinguishable from a broken page. A CHECK written now could only compare a kind against nothing. Item (e) is where types and branches become data and where the refusal belongs; what (b) guarantees is that every brokerage office already HAS a kind for (e) to check against. **NULL EXACTLY WHEN IT IS AN INSURANCE COMPANY, held by a CHECK in BOTH DIRECTIONS.** `Organization_broker_kind_matches_type` refuses a brokerage office with no kind AND an insurer that carries one — proven by attempting all four shapes in rolled-back transactions: both wrong shapes `ERROR: violates check constraint`, both right shapes `INSERT 0 1`. A one-sided constraint would have missed the second, and "broker kind: CORPORATE" on an insurer is a value nothing reads, which is a value somebody eventually reads. **THE CONSTRAINT IS THE ONLY GUARD, AND THAT WAS MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED**: the column is nullable because NULL is correct for an insurer, which makes it OPTIONAL in Prisma's `CreateInput` — so adding it broke exactly ONE typecheck (the in-memory `Organization` fixture, which uses the full model type) and **none of the seven `organization.create` sites**. The compiler asked for the one place it could; the database refuses the seven it could not. Both sets are patched, and the asymmetry is written at the call sites so nobody later "tidies" the comment away. `db:divergence` is structurally blind to CHECK constraints — a recorded blind spot of that gate — so the migration's own assertions are the only thing between this constraint and a future migration dropping it silently. **THE BACKFILL IS AN ASSUMPTION AND IS LABELLED AS ONE.** The CHECK requires a value for every existing brokerage office, so one had to be chosen: CORPORATE, on two grounds — it is what the rows ARE (both dev offices are named as companies; db-test's is a fixture), and it is the NON-RESTRICTING value, so it cannot retroactively invalidate a licence scope, where NATURAL_PERSON would have asserted a one-type limit nobody was asked about. **The residual risk is named in the migration rather than left to be discovered**: if a real office IS a sole establishment and is recorded CORPORATE, item (e)'s refusal will not fire for it — which is a question for the office, not something a migration can measure, and is why a NEW office must state its kind rather than inherit a default. **FOUR MIGRATION ASSERTIONS, ALL FOUR PLANTED AND ALL FOUR INDEPENDENTLY REACHABLE** (unlike item (a), where one had to be labelled defensive), each run against the migration's **own DO block extracted verbatim**: the CHECK dropped → *"does not exist"*; the predicate loosened to name one column → *"no longer names both columns — it reads CHECK ((("brokerKind" IS NOT NULL) OR true))"*; a third enum label → *"expected exactly NATURAL_PERSON,CORPORATE — found …,PARTNERSHIP"*; a brokerage office nulled behind a weakened constraint → *"2 row(s) have a kind that does not match their organization type"*. **AND THE SHARPEST PLANT IS THE ONE THAT PROVES A TEST INSUFFICIENT.** `broker-kind.e2e-spec.ts` checks that the constraint's predicate still NAMES both columns — so the plant replaced it with `CHECK (TRUE OR ("type" IS NOT NULL AND "brokerKind" IS NULL))`, which names both and refuses nothing. **That test SURVIVED while both refusal tests died**, which is exactly the point: the definition check is secondary, the refusal tests are the guard, and the plant is what tells those two apart. Dropping the constraint outright kills all three. **Item (a)'s leak lesson was applied from the start and verified**: every write here throws a sentinel after its INSERT rather than relying on the INSERT failing, so both plants left **0 rows** behind in the cumulative db-test — where item (a)'s equivalent test leaked one and blocked its own constraint from being restored. **Verification**: applied to dev AND db-test; `db:checksums` and `db:divergence` green on both; typecheck api + db exit 0; lint 3/3; api unit **249 files / 3165 tests**; api e2e **7 files / 110 tests**; **from-empty check run before committing** — 122 migrations from nothing, then the seed, the seeded office reading `BROKERAGE_OFFICE / CORPORATE`, then dropped. | **An `Organization` row now has TWO kind columns and they are not independent**: `type` is NOT NULL with no default, and `brokerKind` is NULL exactly when `type` is `INSURANCE_COMPANY`. State both — and expect the DATABASE rather than the compiler to refuse a missing `brokerKind`, because a nullable column is optional in `CreateInput`. **When you add a nullable column whose absence is still an error, the CHECK is not belt-and-braces — it is the only control**, and a test that asserts the constraint merely EXISTS or merely names its columns does not test it: plant `TRUE OR (…)` and watch which of your tests survive. **Do not write the art. 3/a one-type rule against `brokerKind` alone** — it needs item (e)'s licence scope, and the migration says so in its own header so the next reader does not implement half of it. **Every new `Organization` CHECK makes `broker-kind.e2e-spec.ts`'s SQLSTATE assertion less specific** — it asserts the constraint NAME alongside `23514` for exactly that reason, since items (d)–(g) add more office-level rules. |

Both of today's findings came from there. **A generator's output is not evidence about the generator**: mine produced valid TypeScript that passed `tsc` and had silently dropped an export 86 spec files import — only reading the first `git diff` could catch that. **`npm run db:fixture:permissions` now exists** — the web e2e's permission grid said "GENERATED … regenerate rather than hand-edit" for months with no generator behind it; `verify.sh` gates the `--check` form. Read a generator's first diff: mine silently dropped an export 86 spec files import. **Read `IMPROVEMENTS.md`'s new opening principle** before the numbered entries: *prefer the form that cannot express the mistake over the form that requires you to notice it.* |
| 2026-09-19 | **Custom office-scoped RBAC, PHASE 3 of 5 — an office can now define its own roles, and two Highly Confidential reveals stopped riding on the permission that lets you read the record.** Nine commits; 177 permission codes became 182. **`/settings/roles` is the screen the whole rework exists for**: the office's own catalogue, a matrix over all 182 codes grouped by module, create/rename/retire/reactivate. `Role` gained `status` and `isSystem`; there is deliberately **no DELETE** — the `UserRoleAssignment` and `RolePermission` rows pointing at a role ARE the record of who held what and when — and `status` is enforced in every query that DECIDES access, with **FOUR routes into the last-administrator lockout guard** now guarded under the same per-office advisory lock — revoking a grant and deactivating the user (Phase 2), retiring a role, and **removing `user.manage` from a role via the matrix**, which I missed on the first pass and found by asking what else the new screen can take away: unchecking a box does not look like an access action, but an office that ends up with nobody holding `user.manage` cannot grant it back. **`isSystem` GRANTS NOTHING**, proven by a test that holds an `isSystem` role with zero grants and gets 403 everywhere; planting `if (user.roles.length > 0) return true` in `PermissionsGuard` is what proved that test can fail. **Every office got an `OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR`** (22 codes, `isSystem`, strict MFA), granted to every user who could already administer users — asked as a CAPABILITY, never by role name — and **in addition to** their legacy role, never instead. Its grants are a verified strict subset of the legacy administrator's, which is what makes the migration a provably empty per-user effective-permission diff. A NEW office gets that role and **no business roles at all**; nothing in the application creates an Organization (the only two writers are the seed and the demo script), so the rule lives in both writers plus a test asserting an Organization with no active route to user administration is a defect. **The reveal splits differ deliberately.** `employee.manage` → `employee.read`/`.create`/`.update` + `employee.national-id.reveal` (per ROUTE: its DTO accepts only that field); `customer.national-id.reveal` split out of `customer.360-view.read` **per FIELD**, because the same endpoint reveals a phone number a Sales Officer needs for their own customer — a route-level gate would have stopped an officer phoning their own client. Compliance alone holds both; the administrator and the Manager both lose the employee one, which is the point. KYC proven intact end to end. **THREE REAL DEFECTS THE WORK FOUND.** (1) **The AC1 measurement exposed grant drift in the demo office**: the first diff was empty on db-test but had ONE row on dev, because `seed.ts` grants permissions to the DEFAULT office's roles only — so every code added after a demo office was created never reached it, and Office B's administrator role was 3 codes behind the grid (25 across all eight of its roles). Fixed at the source with a mirror-from-the-default-office helper; with the drift closed the dev diff is empty too. (2) **`/settings/users` had no web e2e at all, and its grant dropdown was broken**: the options carried the role NAME while `grantRole` posts that value as `roleId`, which the API validates with `@IsUUID()` — every grant an administrator made from that dropdown was a 400, and the default (set from the first catalogue id) matched no option. Found by writing the test the screen never had. (3) A lint rule caught the catalogue fetch setting state synchronously inside an effect; the house async-IIFE pattern is what it wants. **Two decisions**: the matrix **warns and saves** on classify+co-sign (a small office may want that, and `assertDifferentActors` still refuses a co-sign by whoever recorded it), and the two MFA attributes **are** editable behind a step-up challenge — the first consumer of `@RequireStepUp()` anywhere — because not exposing them would leave every custom role demanding MFA forever. **Verification**: typecheck 6/6, lint 3/3, build 3/3; unit **api 2998 · web 69 · db 36 (+12)**; i18n parity 58/58 (+53 keys, both languages); api e2e **83/83 spec files, 562 tests** across eight batches with the ran-file list diffed against the spec list (new `national-id-reveal-split` 5/5, `office-administrator` 6/6, `last-administrator-lock` 15/15); web e2e **505/505 (433 + 72 @a11y)**, new `roles.spec.ts` 11/11 and `users.spec.ts` 7/7. **AC1: per-user effective-permission diff across the administrator migration EMPTY on both databases** — 36,688 users on db-test, 31 on dev. Every new guard was proven by planting the regression and watching it fire: fourteen plants across the two reveal gates, the grid invariants, the orphaned office, the 23rd grant, the `isSystem` bypass, both UI reveal gates, the segregation warning, the step-up gate, the original dropdown bug restored, the fourth lockout route un-guarded, and an office's own role marked `isSystem`. | **A strict-subset grant set is what makes a "grant everyone this new role" migration provably safe.** Adding `insurer.relationship.manage` to the administrator would have broken that property, which is why it is deferred with the insurer backlog rather than for tidiness — `permissions.spec.ts` now asserts the subset so it cannot quietly stop holding. **The seed grants to the DEFAULT office only.** Any code added after an office was created never reaches that office's roles; the demo script now mirrors the default office's grants, and a future org-provisioning path must do the same or its offices drift silently. **An untested screen is where a half-migrated API lands.** `/settings/users` survived the move to id-addressed assignment with a name-valued dropdown because nothing exercised it; both admin screens now have specs. **Select controls by a `data-*` hook, not by index or label** — the matrix sorts by module then code, so an index follows the catalogue, and every role label appears in the fieldset, the table and every row's dropdown (five matches for one `getByText`). **`getByRole(name)` still matches case-insensitive SUBSTRINGS**: the matrix heading "Permissions for Claims Triage Desk" matched a module heading of "claims". **Two role-name sites remain by design** (SLA escalation routing + the bell recipients that follow it, and the Executive nav order) — they answer "which business function owns this", and the honest fix is Phase 4's routing table. **The permission cache is per-process and that now matters more**: editing a role used to mean editing the seed and redeploying, and is now a button — recorded in `permissions.service.ts`, with the fix named (a shared invalidation channel, not a shorter TTL). **Still to come**: Phase 4 (the unified screen's org-structure half, subdomain resolution, the business-function routing table), Phase 5 (migration rehearsal, dropping the `RoleName` enum type and the pre-migration rows). Insurer CRUD remains specification-only. |
| 2026-09-18 | **Custom office-scoped RBAC, PHASE 2 of 5 — no authorization decision reads a role NAME any more, and the two controls that failed OPEN are gone.** Ten workstreams over ten commits; 167 permission codes became 177. **The MFA pair is now a Role ATTRIBUTE, not a permission** — `Role.requiresMfaAlways` / `requiresHardwareToken`, defaulting to the STRICT value, because a permission could be granted away from the very Role screen Phase 3 adds. The migration backfills only the roles that must be RELAXED, so anything it does not recognise fails closed. **`@RequireRoles` and `RolesGuard` are deleted outright**: all 19 routes (not 17 — I had miscounted) already carried an equivalent `@RequirePermissions`, and each of those seven codes is granted to exactly one role, which is what makes the removal provably zero-delta. Cross-owner visibility became seven `<family>.all-owners.read` codes — named that way, not `.view-all-owners`, because `permissions.spec.ts` proves the External Auditor read-only BY CONSTRUCTION from the `.read`/`.view` suffix and widening that check would have traded a real safety property for a nicer code. The segregation signal keys on 13 checker PERMISSIONS and stays DETECTIVE; the reviewer pool keys on `access-recertification.review` with a second `...review.routine` code preserving its two tiers; `incident.classify` splits three ways; role names validate as bounded strings. **FOUR DEFECTS THE TESTS FOUND THAT READING DID NOT.** (1) `seed.ts` upserts grants and never REMOVES one, so narrowing `incident.classify` in the grid left every existing database with Executive Management still holding it — reseeding does not revoke. Migration `20261004120000` withdraws exactly that grant, scoped to the legacy role name; the seed was deliberately NOT made to prune, because under custom roles an office's own grants are precisely the rows absent from the grid. (2) `pg_advisory_xact_lock` returns `void` and Prisma cannot deserialize a void column — `$queryRaw` made every administrator revoke a 500; `$executeRaw` is the call. (3) A lock guard that threw SYNCHRONOUSLY out of a Promise-returning method. (4) A new e2e created a second Organization and leaked it, producing 17 failures in other files — `signup` refuses with two orgs, and the teardown could never have worked because `AuditLogEntry` is immutable by trigger. **TWO OF MY OWN TESTS COULD NOT FAIL, and planting found both.** The reviewer tiering test passed against a flattened pool because the mock listed the routine reviewer first and `pickReviewer` takes the first eligible id either way; the concurrent-revoke race test passed with the lock REMOVED, on I/O timing alone. Both were fixed — the mock now lists the fallback reviewer first, and the lock has a deterministic serialisation test that cannot pass by luck. **Verification**: typecheck 6/6, lint 3/3, build 3/3; unit **api 2994 (+11) · web 69 · db 28 (+4)**; api e2e **79/79 spec files, 527 tests** across seven batches with the ran-file list diffed against the spec list; contract 4/4; web Playwright **414/414**; a11y **70/70**. AC1 gate: per-user effective-permission diff before vs after, **0 unexplained differences** on both databases — every change is one of the 10 added codes or the 1 deliberate withdrawal. Seed verified on an EMPTY database twice, and the role-permission grid is **byte-identical (382 grants, one md5) across seeded-from-empty, dev and db-test**, which is what proves a migrated database and a freshly seeded one are indistinguishable. | **An `md5` gate is the wrong shape for a phase that adds permissions.** AC1 as written asked for byte-identical effective permissions; this phase adds 10 codes by design, so that could never pass and reporting it as met would have been false. The honest gate is every difference CLASSIFIED — 0 unexplained — which is what was run. **The web e2e has a THIRD copy of the permission grid** (`apps/web/e2e/fixtures/role-permissions.ts`); its header says regenerate rather than hand-edit, and a stale copy broke 4 Playwright tests in three files. Regenerate it from the seeded database whenever a grant moves. **Four role-name decisions REMAIN, deliberately**: SLA escalation targets, the bell routing that follows them, and the Executive nav order — they answer "which business function owns this", not "who may do this", and the honest fix is a routing table in Phase 4. **Phase 3 can now proceed**: nothing fails open, a custom role can be created and assigned, and a custom administrator role is guarded. Before it, decide two things this phase deliberately did not: whether `role.manage` / `permission.manage` should split into `.read` + `.manage` (they gate read-only GETs today and the matrix will read wrong), and whether role assignment should move from name-addressed to id-addressed. **`OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR` is still unseeded** — the 20-code proposal is in the Phase 2 plan artifact and awaits confirmation; `user.manage` is already the anchor three workstreams key on. Insurer CRUD remains specification-only, as instructed. |
| 2026-09-18 | **Custom office-scoped RBAC, PHASE 1 of 5 — roles became per-office data, and authorization stopped resolving by name. Two latent cross-tenant leaks were live the moment the constraint changed, and a third bug locked every user out of everything until it was found.** `Role.name` was globally `UNIQUE`; it is now `@@unique([organizationId, name])`, plus `organizationId`/`nameAr`/`nameEn` and RLS. That constraint change is what made the old lookups a leak rather than a style problem: `PermissionRepository.findCodesForRoles` filtered `role: { name: { in } }`, which once two offices can each define a "Manager" returns BOTH rows and hands the caller their **UNION**; and `PermissionsService` cached under `[...roleNames].sort().join(',')`, serving one office's answer to the other for up to a minute. Both now key on `roleId` (uuids, unique across every office), and `AuthenticatedUser` carries `roleIds` beside the names it keeps for display. **The third bug is the one worth reading.** The first migration gave `RolePermission` an RLS policy scoped through a JOIN to `Role`, specifically to avoid denormalizing `organizationId` where a drifted copy would be the row granting access. That policy can never be satisfied: `tenantScopeExtension` sets `app.current_org_id` **only for models that CARRY `organizationId`**, so `current_setting` was always NULL, every permission read returned empty, and users authenticated fine and then 403'd everywhere — `/auth/me` showed `roles: ["SALES_RELATIONSHIP_OFFICER"]` beside `permissions: 0`. A second migration adds the column and answers the drift worry **structurally** instead: a composite FK `(roleId, organizationId)` → `Role(id, organizationId)` makes a disagreeing grant fail to INSERT, proven by attempting one. **MIGRATION preserved access exactly.** The 11 shared rows are ADOPTED by the lowest-id organization using each and COPIED for the others — so the adopting office's `UserRoleAssignment` rows never move — and an office gets only the roles it actually used: `default` keeps 11, `demo-office-b` gets exactly its 8, with no DPO/Executive/External-Auditor row it never had. The blocking gate was a per-user effective-permission diff before vs after: **empty, 31/31 users, byte-identical md5**. **Verification**: typecheck 6/6, lint 3/3, build 3/3; unit **api 2983 (+13) · web 69 · db 24**; api e2e **75/75 spec files** green across seven batches (`tenant-isolation` 33/33, +6 new), contract 4/4.  **CI then caught a second thing every local gate had missed: `prisma/seed.ts` was typechecked by NOTHING.** `packages/db/tsconfig.json` sets `"include": ["src"]`, so the seed and its seed-data modules were outside the program — `npm run typecheck` passed 6/6 across the monorepo while the seed still wrote the pre-migration `Role` shape and could not run at all. It failed on the one CI step no local script covers, on a database fresh enough to need a CREATE rather than an upsert-existing. A new typecheck-only `packages/db/tsconfig.seed.json` (a second config, because the build's `rootDir: "src"` makes widening the existing `include` a TS6059 error) is now wired into `db`'s `typecheck` — and it immediately caught a real defect in the very fix that motivated it: the edit that added `nameEn`/`nameAr` to `RoleSeed` had dropped `description`, which ran fine because types are erased. Verified by dropping and recreating a scratch database, migrating and seeding it from empty, twice. | **An RLS policy on a table the extension does not scope is not defence in depth — it is an outage waiting for a deploy.** The scoped set is derived from the DMMF as "carries `organizationId`", so a policy reading `app.current_org_id` on any other table matches nothing, forever. `tenant-scope.extension.spec.ts` now guards this: every RLS-protected model must be in `TENANT_SCOPED_MODELS`. **`tenant-scope.extension.ts` itself was never touched** — adding the column is what brings a model under Layer 1, which is the whole argument for deriving the set from the schema. **Two lists still FAIL OPEN and are Phase 2's first job**: `ALWAYS_MFA_ROLES` and `PRIVILEGED_ROLES` match on role name, so a custom role silently qualifies for the trusted-device MFA skip. Not yet exploitable — no custom role can exist until Phase 3's Role screen — so **Phase 2 must land before Phase 3**. The five visibility lists fail CLOSED by contrast, and the recertification reviewer pool would refuse to start a cycle for a custom-role-only office. **`maker-checker.util.ts` is untouched and needs no change**: 62 call sites compare user IDs, backed by 17 DB CHECK constraints, so multi-role cannot weaken separation. **`RoleName` the enum TYPE is deliberately kept** (nothing references it) — dropping it and the legacy rows is deferred a release so rollback stays cheap. **The seed is now typechecked — keep it that way.** `packages/db` runs a second `tsc` against `tsconfig.seed.json`; every remaining phase touches both the schema and the seed, and without it a broken seed reaches CI looking locally green. **Also run `npm run db:format` before pushing any schema change**: CI's gate is "formatting leaves no diff" and no local script runs it, which cost one red round-trip on a single line of field alignment. **Verify a seed change on an EMPTY database**, not the dev one — an upsert against rows a migration already created exercises the update path and never the create path, which is exactly the half that was broken. Still to come: Phase 2 (convert the 12 role-name-keyed decision points), Phase 3 (`status`/`isSystem`, Role CRUD + matrix, OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR), Phase 4 (unified User/Employee screen), Phase 5 (migration rehearsal + the remaining tests). |
| 2026-09-17 | **The backup restore drill had failed every scheduled run since 2026-08-31 — three stacked faults, each hidden behind the one before it, and the third was the drill lying about what it verified.** **Fault 1 — the seed crashed.** Nothing in this job ever ran `prisma generate`: `npm ci` has no postinstall hook, and unlike `ci.yml` this workflow runs no turbo task (turbo's graph builds `@ibms/db`, which is where `ci.yml` quietly gets its client). `RoleName` was `undefined` and the seed died on `Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'SALES_RELATIONSHIP_OFFICER')`. Fixed with a standalone `npx prisma generate` — no dotenv wrapper and no `DATABASE_URL` (generate only reads the schema); `npm run db:generate` would NOT work here because that script is `dotenv -e ../../.env` and this workflow only writes `.env.test`. **Fault 2 — there was no passphrase.** The repository has **zero** Actions secrets, so `BACKUP_DRILL_ENCRYPTION_KEY` was never set and the drill step would have exited 1 even once the seed was fixed. Fixing only fault 1 would have moved the red one step later. Now generates an EPHEMERAL per-run passphrase, secret still overriding when present. **Fault 3, the real find — the verification compared ESTIMATES and called the drift data loss.** It summed `pg_stat_user_tables.n_live_tup` after an `ANALYZE`, with a comment asserting that made the comparison "exact, not approximate". `ANALYZE` SAMPLES; `n_live_tup` stays an estimate. Proved rather than assumed, on ONE database with no restore involved: estimate **4,142,466** vs true count **4,141,630** — an 836-row phantom. Replaced with an exact per-table `count(*)` via `query_to_xml`, placed AFTER `END_EPOCH` so verification time is never charged against the RTO target; verified there are no partitioned or inherited tables (0 and 0, 130 base tables) that could double-count. **Verification**: the drill now runs locally end to end — **PASS**, 4,141,630 original vs 4,141,630 restored, 638s against the 900s RTO target, exit 0. | **Fixing the first failure in a stack tells you nothing about the second.** Three weeks of red were three different bugs; the third only became visible once the first two were fixed and the drill could reach its own summary. Check what comes AFTER the failing step before calling it fixed. **A comment asserting a property is not the property** — "ANALYZE makes this exact" was wrong, and one query against a single database disproved it in seconds. **Do not weaken a check that might be catching something real**: the estimate-vs-exact measurement is what established the FAIL was the metric and not the restore, and the fixed drill then matched that exact number on both sides. **The repo has NO Actions secrets** — any workflow written to require one has never run. **An ephemeral passphrase is the right key for this drill**, not a missing long-lived one: it proves the round trip against a throwaway seeded database and keeps a real credential out of a job with no real data to protect. |

## Cloning this repo

```bash
git clone --recurse-submodules https://github.com/SHOUQALMAHARBAH/IBMS-APP.git
# already cloned without it?
git submodule update --init --recursive
```

The submodule pins a specific `ibms-brain` commit — it does not auto-track `main`. Pull
in newer brain rules deliberately:

```bash
cd ibms-brain && git pull origin main && cd ..
git add ibms-brain && git commit -m "ibms-brain: sync to latest"
```

## What's here today

Infrastructure (Part A + Part B), plus Part C **Domain A, Processes 1–10** — Lead
Management (#1), Prospect Management (#2), Customer Acquisition/Onboarding (#3-4, with
*simulated* screening), Needs Assessment (#5), Risk Assessment (#6), Product
Recommendation / Program Design (#7), Cross-Selling (#8, a no-op until the Policy module
lands), Up-Selling (#9, flags a survey that outgrew the designed property Sum Insured),
Relationship Management / CRM (#10, logs every touchpoint as an `Interaction` and serves
the aggregated 360° customer timeline — policies/claims/complaints empty until Domains
B/C/E land). **Domain A is complete.** Part C **Domain B** has begun with RFQ / Market
Submission (#11) — a minimal `Opportunity` parent (created from a FINALIZED Insurance
Program) plus `RFQ`/`RFQInsurer` (one RFQ per line, insurer shortlist, per-insurer
response tracking, a nightly business-day follow-up alert sweep). This paragraph predates
Domain B #12–22, Domains C–H and Parts D–F, all of which have since been built —
**root `README.md` § Scope status is the authority, not this summary.** Part F's
bilingual UI is complete: all 93 screens — the 89 under `app/(app)/` and the four
`(auth)` screens that render before sign-in — read from
`apps/web/lib/i18n/translations/`, including the 403 permission-denied branch
(see § What's New). Part G's final verification
checklist is complete — all seven items closed and guarded. See § Known gaps for
each built item's deferred edges.

**Custom office-scoped RBAC is IN PROGRESS — Phases 1, 2 and 3 of 5 have landed.** The
fixed 11-role catalogue is gone as an architecture: `Role` carries `organizationId`, a
role name is unique per office rather than globally, and authorization resolves
permissions from `roleId` — never from a role name, because two offices may each define
a "Manager" with different grants. `Permission` stays a single global catalogue; only
the grants are per-office. The legacy 11 survive as ordinary per-office rows carrying
their old machine names, so nobody's access changed. An office now DEFINES its own roles
at `/settings/roles`, every office has an `OFFICE_ADMINISTRATOR`, and a new office gets
that role and nothing else.

Before touching anything role-related read **`docs/office-scoped-rbac.md`** — it
carries these four constraints in full, the things that will bite, and the Phase 4
hand-off:

1. **A role NAME is not an identity.** `PermissionRepository.findCodesForRoles` and
   `PermissionsService` both take ids, deliberately — a name-keyed lookup returns every
   same-named role across every office and hands the caller their union. The same
   applies at the API boundary: role assignment is id-addressed
   (`{ roleId }` / `{ roleIds }`), and the by-name repository lookups were deleted, not
   left dormant.
2. **An RLS policy is only enforceable on a model carrying `organizationId`**, because
   that is how `tenantScopeExtension` decides whether to set `app.current_org_id` at
   all. A policy on any other table matches nothing, forever.
3. **`Role.status` has to reach every query that DECIDES access**, not just the screen.
   A retired role that still satisfied the authorization read or the
   last-administrator count would keep granting everything it ever granted.
4. **`isSystem` grants nothing.** It stops the Role screen renaming, retiring or
   re-granting a row and is read nowhere else. A flag called "system" on a Role is
   exactly where an `if (isSystem) allow` bypass gets smuggled in; there is a test
   whose only job is to prove it does not exist.

Phases 4 and 5 (the unified screen's org-structure half, subdomain resolution, the
business-function routing table that retires the two remaining role-name sites; then
migration rehearsal and dropping the `RoleName` enum type) are not built.

**Insurer CRUD HAS shipped** — API and screens, with `insurer.create`, `insurer.update`, `insurer.deactivate`,
`insurer.read`, `insurer.directory.read` and `insurer.office-form.map` granted. **CORRECTED 2026-10-19: this said `insurer.relationship.manage`, WHICH DOES NOT EXIST** — Phase 1 split that umbrella five ways because it gated two ENTITIES (an office's insurer records and the insurance LINES those records carry), and the sentence was never updated. Anybody handed this file would have gone looking for a code that is not in the graph. This
paragraph said it was "deliberately deferred" for the whole branch that built it, which
is the doc rot `meta/lex/definition-of-done.md` names: a deferral is true when written
and nobody revisits it. What survives of that deferral is the property it protected —
those codes sit OUTSIDE the office administrator's strict-subset-at-migration set and are
listed explicitly in `ADDED_AFTER_THE_MIGRATION`, so a fifth cannot slip in without a
decision.

**Before touching anything insurer-related read `docs/insurer-management.md`** — the
eleven constraints in full, including the two that are easiest to break by accident: the
canonical name key lives in the DATABASE and the TypeScript mirror is for suggestions
only, and an insurer row holds two kinds of fact where only the company-level half may
ever leave the office. Its two siblings go deeper on the global catalogue
(`docs/insurer-master-registry.md`) and the cross-office directory
(`docs/insurer-directory.md`).

## Common commands

```bash
npm install
cp .env.example .env
docker compose up -d db
npm run db:migrate:dev
npm run db:seed       # per-office roles + the global permission catalogue — RBAC needs these
npm run dev          # web:3000, api:4000
npm run lint
npm run typecheck
npm run test          # vitest, web + api

# api e2e — separate test DB (db-test), never the dev DB above. See README
# § Dev DB vs. test DB.
cp .env.test.example .env.test
docker compose up -d db-test
npm run db:test:migrate:dev
npm run test:e2e

npm run e2e           # playwright + axe-core, web

# Demo data for two Organizations, through the real HTTP API — DEV DB only,
# never .env.test. DEMO_PASSWORD is REQUIRED and has no default: the script
# refuses to start without one, because the password it used to carry was a
# literal in this repo. Sign in with it, then pair an authenticator at
# Settings -> Security. See apps/api/scripts/README-SEED-DEMO.md.
$env:DEMO_PASSWORD = (Read-Host "Choose a demo password")   # PowerShell
npm run seed:demo -w api

# Releases the demo logins from an MFA prompt nobody can answer — the repair
# for "I entered the correct code and it says the code is wrong" on an account
# the seed enrolled with a secret it never showed anyone. Passwords untouched.
npm run demo:release -w api

# The SAME repair for the sample.* accounts, which demo:release cannot reach:
# it matches the `demo.` prefix only, so it reports "No demo account is locked"
# and changes nothing. Four of eleven were locked this way on 2026-10-11.
npm run sample:release -w api
```

**Every screen is held to `docs/frontend-ux-directive.md`** — the owner's standing frontend and UX
instruction, in the repo because it reached one session as a message and the next would not have seen
it. Read it before building a screen. Its four-state requirement, its rule that a control a role
cannot use does not exist on their screen, and its reservation of implementation detail to the
System/Security Administrator are the three that change code most often. **It overlaps
`docs/b7-consistency-record.md` and disagrees with it in four places** — the header of the directive
states which, and those four are the owner's to settle, not to merge.

**Handing the system to someone who does not read code: `docs/first-run.md`** — the
bilingual (AR-first) first-sign-in walkthrough, walked against a running stack rather than
written from the source. It carries the measured per-account permission table, so if the
seed's role grants move, re-measure it rather than editing it by hand.

## Environment

- Node `20.19.0` — see `.nvmrc`. This satisfies Prisma 7's Node floor (≥20.19/22.12/24)
  but Prisma itself stays pinned at `6.19.3` in `package.json` — Node version no longer
  blocks a Prisma 7 install, so don't bump `prisma`/`@prisma/client` past 6.x without
  doing the driver-adapter (`@prisma/adapter-pg`) + `prisma.config.ts` migration first.
  See the Prisma note in root `README.md`.
- Docker required for Postgres locally and for building `apps/api`/`apps/web` images.

## Repo map

```
apps/web/     Next.js frontend
apps/api/     NestJS backend
packages/db/  Shared Prisma schema + client (@ibms/db)
ibms-brain/   Submodule — standards/rules/context (loaded via the import above)
.claude/      Agents/commands mirrored from ibms-brain + a subset of its hooks — see
              README.md § .claude/ — agents, commands, hooks
```

`@code-reviewer` and `@software-developer` (defined in `ibms-brain/meta/agents/`) and
`/brain-gap` are available in this repo via `.claude/`. Not yet wired here: the `git push`
evidence-gate hook (`enforce-evidence.sh`) and the domain-code hooks — see README.md for why.

## Before you write code

Same rule as ibms-brain: read `meta/context/` for the area, `meta/lex/` for what's
mandatory, cite the source document (PDPL / CBJ / ISO 27001/27701 / a specific
`PRIV-STD-*`/`PRIV-SOP-*`) in the PR when the change touches a regulatory obligation.
